  "file_text": "# M-IRT Research Report: Foundations for Reference-Free, Contamination-Resilient LLM Evaluation\n\n*Artifact: `gen_plan_research_1_idx2` (literature-grounded design parameters for the M-IRT experiment). Date: 2026-09-05.*\n\n---\n\n### 1. Executive Summary\n\nM-IRT (Metamorphic Item Response Theory) inverts the field's \"obvious\" axiom \u2014 that trustworthy LLM evaluation requires a frozen, held-out, externally-graded test set \u2014 by combining (a) **on-the-fly, procedurally generated Metamorphic Item Clusters (MICs)** that yield multiple graded responses per latent question; (b) **Samejima's Graded Response Model (GRM)** [5] fit via Bock\u2013Aitkin marginal maximum likelihood over cluster-level consistency grades; and (c) a **simulated contamination protocol** [7, 8] that mirrors HELM's \"train-test contamination\" framing [1] and Sainz et al.'s TS-Guessing diagnostic [8]. Unlike ATLAS [2] (which uses 3PL on a fixed calibrated item bank of >3,000 LLMs), Lost-in-Benchmarks / PSN-IRT [3] (4PL with a neural calibration on a 41,871-item binary response matrix), tinyBenchmarks [4] (Rasch/IRT++ for accuracy estimation only), and GRM-for-LLM-Judges [6] (GRM for LLM-as-judge reliability), M-IRT requires **no ground-truth answer key, no fixed calibrated item bank, and no pre-existing LLM population**: every cluster is generated, every grade is computed from the model's own outputs, and contamination is detected by consistency violation. M-IRT's defensibility rests on three pre-existing foundations the experiment inherits verbatim: Samejima GRM with ordered thresholds for polytomous rating data [5, 6]; Logic-Grounded Metamorphic Testing (LGMT) [10]'s FOL-derived input transformations (De Morgan, contraposition, quantifier swap) for the *negation* and *contrapositive* MRs; and TS-Guessing's masking-based contamination detection [8]. Recommended estimator settings (Gauss-Hermite quadrature, 41 nodes on \u03b8 \u2208 [\u22124, 4], Bock-Aitkin EM, max |\u0394\u03b1|, |\u0394\u03b2_k| < 1e-4), MR set (paraphrase, negation, contrapositive, addition of distractor), reasoning-domain generators (PrOntoQA's PCFG over a typed ontology [11], GSM8K/MAWPS's arithmetic word problems [12, 13], LogicBench's syllogistic 25-pattern catalog [14], FOLIO's FOL-annotated NL pairs [15]), and a contamination simulation that injects (q, a*) into the prompt of one cluster member and measures \u0394(static_accuracy) vs. \u0394(\u03b8_M-IRT) are all specified in the body below.\n\n---\n\n### 2. IRT-for-LLM Positioning Matrix\n\nThe four anchor papers differ on exactly the dimensions M-IRT needs to differentiate on. The matrix below summarises them with verbatim quotes and numbers.\n\n| Paper | IRT family | Ground-truth needed for fitting? | Reported r / MAE with static benchmark | Contamination-tested? | Sample size |\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| **ATLAS** [2] (arXiv:2511.04689, ICML 2026 Spotlight) | **3PL** with discrimination *a_i*, difficulty *b_i*, guessing *c_i* (Birnbaum 1968). \"We adopt the three-parameter logistic (3PL) IRT model (Birnbaum, 1968; Lord, 2012): p_i(\u03b8_\u2113) = c_i + (1\u2212c_i) / (1 + exp(\u2212a_i(\u03b8_\u2113\u2212b_i))).\" | **Yes** \u2014 calibrated on HuggingFace Open LLM Leaderboard using observed binary responses Y_i,\u2113 \u2208 {0, 1}. | Reconstructed accuracy \"closely matches raw accuracies across all five benchmarks.\" MAE on HellaSwag = 0.157 with 41 items (vs. 5,600). MAE on TruthfulQA = 0.064 with 48 items. | Only as a general motivation (\"contamination sensitivity\" in static benchmarks). No simulated contamination protocol. | 3,000+ LLMs, 5 benchmarks. |\n| **Lost in Benchmarks? / PSN-IRT** [3] (arXiv:2505.15055, AAAI 2026 Oral) | **4PL** neural PSN architecture: P(X=1\\|\u03b8) = c + (d\u2212c)/(1+e^{\u2212a(\u03b8\u2212b)}). Adds a \"feasibility\" parameter *d* to 3PL. | **Yes** \u2014 trained end-to-end on observed binary (Model, Item, Outcome) tuples from 11 benchmarks. | Used IRT-\u03b8 to build smaller benchmarks with \"stronger alignment with human preference\" (qualitative; no single r number in PDF). | Not tested. | 41,871 items, 11 benchmarks, 12 LLMs. |\n| **tinyBenchmarks / IRT++** [4] (arXiv:2402.14992, ICML 2024) | **Rasch / 2PL / neural** for *item selection*, not \u03b8 estimation. \"Clustering these representations allows us to find a more robust evaluation set. Furthermore, using the IRT model, we develop tools for improving benchmark accuracy estimates.\" | **Yes** \u2014 IRT++ is fit on observed binary correctness, then used as a regression prior for accuracy on a held-out slice. | IRT++ predicts MMLU accuracy within **1.9% MAE** using 100 items out of 14K. | Not tested (paper assumes benchmark answers are observable). | ~200 LLMs across 4 benchmarks. |\n| **Diagnosing Reliability of LLM-as-a-Judge via IRT** [6] (arXiv:2602.00521, ICML 2026) | **Samejima GRM** (polytomous). \"For a rating scale with K unique score values, the probability \u2026 P(Y\u2265k\\|\u03b8) = 1/(1+exp(\u2212\u03b1_p(\u03b8 \u2212 \u03b2_pk))).\" | **Yes** \u2014 GRM is fit on observed Likert ratings from LLM judges. | Not applicable \u2014 paper measures *judge reliability*, not static-bench alignment. | Robustness is measured via prompt perturbations (typo, newline, paraphrase), **not** contamination injection. | 7 LLM judges, multiple TopicalChat/HelpSteer-2 criteria. |\n| **M-IRT (this paper, proposed)** | **Samejima GRM** on cluster-level *consistency grades* (0\u20133) instead of Likert ratings. | **No** \u2014 grades computed from the model's own outputs across an MIC's variants. | Pending experiment \u2014 target r \u2265 0.85 with static benchmarks; expected MAE reduction on contaminated vs. uncontaminated split. | **Yes, by construction** \u2014 injection protocol in \u00a73. | Modest: n_models \u2264 10, n_items (MICs) 80\u2013150. |\n\n**M-IRT deltas vs. each anchor:**\n\n- vs. **ATLAS** [2]: ATLAS requires a calibrated, fixed item bank (the *a_i, b_i, c_i* parameters depend on observing 3,000+ LLMs respond to each item) and cannot be re-run on unseen items without re-calibration. M-IRT generates MICs on-the-fly, so the \"item bank\" is refreshed every evaluation session. ATLAS uses 3PL because items are dichotomous; M-IRT uses GRM because cluster-level consistency is naturally ordered (4 grades: fully consistent, partially consistent, contradicted, refusal).\n- vs. **Lost-in-Benchmarks / PSN-IRT** [3]: PSN-IRT's 4PL adds a \"feasibility\" parameter that captures ceiling effects on saturated benchmarks. M-IRT's GRM does not need feasibility because each MIC is freshly generated at a controlled difficulty (PrOntoQA ontology depth, LogicBench rule complexity).\n- vs. **tinyBenchmarks / IRT++** [4]: tinyBenchmarks uses IRT only as a *selection* tool to choose 100 items; the *evaluation* still requires ground-truth accuracy. M-IRT eliminates ground truth by using cluster-level consistency grades.\n- vs. **LLM-as-a-Judge GRM** [6]: that paper's \"intrinsic consistency\" metric (CV < 0.1) is a *validity check* on the judge, not the evaluation target. M-IRT's GRM is fit on the *model being evaluated*, with cluster-level consistency grades as the polytomous response.\n\n---\n\n### 3. Contamination Methodology & Simulation Protocol\n\nHELM [1] flagged the issue in its \u00a7G appendix: *\"We have uneven and usually limited information on the training procedure for these models \u2026 we cannot verify whether the model was trained on the data we evaluate it on \u2026 we flag known instances of model contamination (i.e. where the model creators have disclosed possible contamination for a given scenario) when we report results.\"* HELM treats contamination as an externally reported attribute, not a property a static test can detect \u2014 exactly the limitation M-IRT exploits.\n\nSainz et al. [8] (Investigating Data Contamination, NAACL 2024) operationalise two distinct detection mechanisms: (i) **n-gram overlap** with the pretraining corpus (Brown et al. use 13-gram; GPT-4 uses 50-character) and (ii) **TS-Guessing**, which masks a wrong multiple-choice option or an unlikely word and checks whether the model fills the gap verbatim \u2014 *\"In the MMLU benchmark, ChatGPT and GPT-4 demonstrated an exact match rate of 52% and 57%, respectively, in guessing the missing options in benchmark test data.\"* M-IRT inherits the *guessing* paradigm and generalises it from masked-string recovery to masked-cluster-consistency: the model is asked the same MIC twice, once uncontaminated, once with (q, a*) injected into the prompt.\n\nThe contamination taxonomy from the Benchmark Data Contamination survey [7] (Xu et al., 2024) is relevant: contamination severity scales from *semantic* (same topic) \u2192 *information* (related facts) \u2192 *data* (raw text without labels) \u2192 *label* (full benchmark with gold answers). M-IRT's protocol targets the **label level**, which is the most damaging and the only one a model can plausibly memorise verbatim during pre-training.\n\n**Recommended M-IRT contamination simulation procedure (verbatim for the experiment):**\n\n1. **Baseline (clean) run.** For each MIC, present each model the seed question *q* and the metamorphic variants {m_1(q), \u2026, m_K(q)} independently. Compute the cluster-level consistency grade *g_clean \u2208 {0, 1, 2, 3}* using the rubric in \u00a76.\n2. **Contaminated run.** For each seed *q*, append the gold answer a* (computed by the MIC generator's symbolic solver, not by an external benchmark) into the prompt for *q* only, leaving every metamorphic variant un-contaminated. Re-collect responses. Compute *g_cont*.\n3. **Negative control.** Run the same MICs with a *random* \"gold\" string a*_rand = \"purple elephant 42\" injected; compute *g_neg*. Models that memorise a* verbatim should jump on the contaminated seed but not on the negative control.\n4. **Diagnostic.** Report three quantities per model:\n   - **\u0394_static_accuracy** = (static_acc on q with injection) \u2212 (static_acc on q clean). A model that memorised a* rises here.\n   - **\u0394_\u03b8_M-IRT** = \u03b8_M-IRT (contaminated session) \u2212 \u03b8_M-IRT (clean session). M-IRT should *not* rise because the contaminated answer is local to q and the GRM sees the broken consistency across the cluster.\n   - **Detection rate** = fraction of contaminated clusters where the model gave the verbatim gold in the seeded q but violated consistency on at least one variant. This is the analogue of Sainz et al.'s 52% / 57% [8].\n5. **Success criterion.** \u0394_static_accuracy > 0.1 AND \u0394_\u03b8_M-IRT < 0.05 (within posterior SE) is the operational signature of contamination resistance. The hypothesis is that static accuracy is fooled by injection but M-IRT's cluster-level GRM is not, because the metamorphic variants have no answer-key injection.\n\nThis procedure is *controllable* (we set the injection), *observable* (we measure both static and GRM signals), and *fair* (the same MIC is evaluated in clean and contaminated conditions), directly addressing HELM's [1] explicit call for \"more proactive documentation/disclosure of model contamination \u2026 as well as the use of canaries or other methods for minimizing contamination.\"\n\n---\n\n### 4. Metamorphic Relations in NLP \u2014 Precedent for Reference-Free Evaluation\n\nMetamorphic testing is the field's name for \"oracle-free\" consistency testing. Chen et al.'s ACM Computing Surveys article [16] (2018, \"Metamorphic Testing: A Review of Challenges and Opportunities\") defines an MR as a property of a target function *f* involving multiple inputs \u27e8x_1, \u2026, x_n\u27e9 and outputs \u27e8f(x_1), \u2026, f(x_n)\u27e9: R_i(input) \u21d2 R_o(output). Two recent landmark studies adapt this to LLMs.\n\n**LLMORPH (Cho et al., ICSME 2025) [17]** catalogues 191 MRs across 24 NLP tasks and runs 561,267 test executions on GPT-4, Llama-3, and Hermes-2. Its core example (Figure 1, paper p. 2): *\"The MR in Figure 1 states that, given two inputs where one is a paraphrase of the other, the inference results should be the same.\"* Their reported fault-finding: MT identifies 11% of failures missed by traditional ground-truth testing, with a true-positive rate of ~60%. Most false positives come from *semantic drift* in the paraphraser \u2014 the very problem LGMT solves.\n\n**LGMT (Zhou et al., Knowledge-Based Systems 2026) [10]** is the closest precedent for M-IRT. It grounds MRs in *first-order logic equivalences* \u2014 De Morgan's laws, contraposition, quantifier transformations \u2014 instead of lexical paraphrasing. LGMT constructs 20 FOL-grounded MR types across formula-, symbol-, premise-, and conclusion-level transformations, generating 76,298 candidate metamorphic groups from FOLIO, LogicBench, and LogiQA, and evaluating 3,091 sampled groups across 6 LLMs and 4 prompting strategies. Their headline finding (verbatim): *\"Models are particularly sensitive to symbol-level and conclusion-level variations, and \u2026 Few-shot CoT only partially mitigates these issues.\"*\n\n**Recommended M-IRT MR set (4 MRs, in priority order):**\n\n1. **Paraphrase invariance** [17]. MR: x_2 is a paraphrase of x_1 \u21d2 f(x_1) = f(x_2). Implemented by generating two surface forms of the same MIC seed (controlled lexical substitution at the sentence level, not semantic-drift-prone neural paraphrasing). Justification: the longest-established MR in NLP, with >50 papers in the LLMORPH catalog [17]; prior FP rate is task-dependent but lowest for paraphrase on entailment-style tasks.\n2. **Negation** [10]. MR: f(\u00acx) = \u00acf(x) when *x* is a logical proposition. Implemented by negating a premise (e.g., \"Every cat is a carnivore\" \u2192 \"No cat is a carnivore\") and verifying the entailment direction reverses. Justification: LGMT [10] explicitly identifies \"symbol-level\" and \"conclusion-level\" variations as the highest sensitivity regime; LogicBench [14] shows existing LLMs struggle with inference rules involving negations.\n3. **Contrapositive** [10]. MR: f(A \u2192 B) = f(\u00acB \u2192 \u00acA). Implemented by generating both directions of a conditional and checking that entailment judgments flip in lockstep. Justification: derived from a *proved* FOL equivalence, so the metamorphic relationship is exact by construction \u2014 no semantic drift (this is LGMT's main contribution, and it is exactly what makes M-IRT reference-free).\n4. **Distractor invariance (additive perturbation)** [17]. MR: f(x) = f(x \u2295 d) where *d* is a domain-irrelevant sentence. Implemented by adding a sentence that does not affect the entailment (e.g., a colour or a time-of-day). Justification: classical \"noise robustness\" MR from the LLMORPH catalog; this catches models that latch onto salient tokens rather than reasoning about premises.\n\nMathematical justifications, written so they survive peer review:\n\n- Paraphrase: f(x) = f(rewrite(x)). Two surface forms of the same logical content must yield identical entailment.\n- Negation: f(\u00acx) = \u00acf(x). Negating a single premise must flip the entailment direction on any entailed conclusion.\n- Contrapositive: f(A \u2192 B) = f(\u00acB \u2192 \u00acA). Equivalent logical forms must yield equivalent LLM judgments.\n- Distractor: f(x) = f(x \u2295 d) for d irrelevant to the entailment. Insensitivity to irrelevant content is the operational definition of \"reading the premises.\"\n\nThe LGMT paper [10] explicitly motivates M-IRT's core claim: *\"By validating whether LLMs preserve their judgments across logically equivalent test cases, [consistency-based evaluation] moves evaluation beyond isolated correctness on static benchmarks and toward robustness under logical invariance.\"*\n\n---\n\n### 5. GRM Fitting \u2014 Concrete Estimator Settings\n\nSamejima's GRM [5] (Psychometrika, 1969; cited in [6] as the canonical reference) is the only polytomous IRT model in the LLM-evaluation literature to date. The Choi et al. LLM-as-Judge paper [6] gives the exact likelihood (their Eq. 1):\n\n> *\"P(Y_{pj} \u2265 k | \u03b8_j) = 1 / (1 + exp(\u2212\u03b1_p (\u03b8_j \u2212 \u03b2_{pk})))*\n\nwhere \u03b8_j \u2208 \u211d is the latent quality of subject *j*, \u03b1_p \u2208 \u211d\u207a is discrimination, and {\u03b2_{pk}} is a monotonically increasing sequence of *K* thresholds. The probability of exactly score *k* is the difference of adjacent cumulatives [6]:\n\n> *\"P(Y_{pj} = k | \u03b8_j) = P(Y_{pj} \u2265 k | \u03b8_j) \u2212 P(Y_{pj} \u2265 k+1 | \u03b8_j)\"*\n\nChoi et al. [6] note (verbatim): *\"When K = 2 only one threshold survives and the model collapses to the two-parameter logistic (2PL) form, which is what we use for the binary criteria.\"* For M-IRT with 4 consistency grades (0, 1, 2, 3), K = 4 and the model has 1 \u03b1 and 3 \u03b2 thresholds per item (cluster).\n\n**Implementation settings (recommended):**\n\n| Setting | Value | Rationale |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Response model** | Samejima GRM, polytomous K = 4 | Cluster-level consistency is ordered; binary 2PL loses signal on partial consistency. |\n| **Estimator** | Bock\u2013Aitkin EM with marginal MLE | Standard for IRT in education testing; cited by Baker & Kim (2004) and by all modern IRT packages (mirt, girth). |\n| **Ability prior** | \u03b8 ~ N(0, 1) | Choi et al. [6] use this prior with NUTS sampling; for MML the standard choice is also \u03b8 ~ N(0, 1). |\n| **Quadrature** | Gauss-Hermite, 41 nodes, range [\u22124, 4] | The text-book \"high accuracy\" choice for Bock\u2013Aitkin MML; 41 nodes \u2248 10\u207b\u2074 truncation error on the log-likelihood. |\n| **Convergence** | max \\|\u0394\u03b1\\| < 1e-4 AND max \\|\u0394\u03b2_k\\| < 1e-4 across all items, OR 500 iterations | Standard psychometrics stopping rule (cf. mirt default of 1e-4 with 5000 iters); we tighten iterations for tractability. |\n| **Item parameter priors (Bayesian alternative)** | \u03b1 ~ LogNormal(0, 0.5), \u03b2_k ~ N(0, 1) with ordering \u03b2_k < \u03b2_{k+1} | Choi et al. [6] use these priors with NUTS. Useful for sample sizes < 50 items where MML can be unstable. |\n| **Fit indices** | Limited-information Pearson \u03c7\u00b2 and G\u00b2 (Cai & Hansen, 2013); RMSEA | Standard practice; RMSEA \u2264 0.05 good, \u2264 0.08 acceptable. |\n| **Software** | girth (Python, default MML) or mirt (R via rpy2) | Both implement Bock\u2013Aitkin EM for GRM by default. girth is faster; mirt is more battle-tested. |\n| **Number of clusters (items)** | 50 minimum for stable \u03b1/\u03b2 in polytomous; 80\u2013150 recommended | With n_models \u2264 10, the item-parameter variance scales as 1/(n_models \u00d7 n_items); at n_models = 10, n_items = 80 gives reasonable SE on \u03b1. |\n| **Discrimination pruning** | Drop items with \u03b1 < 0.5 from the final item bank | ATLAS [2] drops items with point-biserial r < 0.1; we apply analogous psychometric filtering on the GRM's \u03b1. |\n| **Marginal reliability** | \u03c1 = Var(\u03b8\u0302) / (Var(\u03b8\u0302) + E[\u03c3\u00b2]) | Choi et al. [6] use this; threshold \u03c1 > 0.7 for reliable measurement. |\n| **Within-rating consistency** | CV < 0.1 across prompt variations | Choi et al. [6] verbatim: *\"We regard CV < 0.1 as acceptable consistency.\"* Adapted to MIC: across paraphrastic variants of the same seed. |\n\n**Sample-size caution.** With n_models < 10 (modest compared to ATLAS's 3,000+), the *item-response matrix* is sparse. Choi et al. [6] flag this explicitly: GRM requires *at least one observation per rating value per item*, so a model that consistently produces the same grade across all variants yields no information on the missing thresholds. M-IRT mitigates this by ensuring each MIC is presented as a multi-model evaluation, so each cluster is graded by *all* models \u2014 yielding n_models \u2265 10 ratings per cluster. We recommend n_models \u2265 10 for M-IRT.\n\n---\n\n### 6. Reasoning Domains for MIC Generation\n\nThe MIC generator should mirror four existing reasoning benchmarks, each chosen for a specific structural property that maps cleanly onto one of the four MRs.\n\n**PrOntoQA (Saparov & He, ICLR 2023) [11].** *Generation grammar.* Each example is generated in four steps from a synthetic world model in first-order logic (Figure 2 of [11]):\n1. Generate an *ontology* (a tree of concepts with optional properties).\n2. Generate a *proof* from the ontology (a sequence of Horn clauses).\n3. Translate the ontology into a natural-language *context* via a controlled grammar.\n4. Translate the proof into a *query*, *chain-of-thought*, and *label*.\n\nThe grammar guarantees a unique proof per question, and the proof is recoverable from the chain-of-thought by a semantic parser \u2014 so \"ground truth\" is **derivable from the generator itself**, not from any external key. Three control variables: *depth* (number of proof steps), *ontology type* (true / false / fictional), *predicate arity* (unary / binary). *Why include:* parametric difficulty + guaranteed derivable ground truth + clean paraphrase and contrapositive variants.\n\n**GSM8K (Cobbe et al. 2021) [12]** and **MAWPS (Koncel-Kedziorski et al. 2016) [13]**. *Item structure.* GSM8K has 8.5K grade-school math word problems (7.5K train + 1K test), each requiring 2\u20138 elementary arithmetic steps. MAWPS is an online repository of math word problems with similar structure. *Metamorphic-friendliness:* adding a distractor sentence preserves the answer; negating a quantity (e.g., \"Alice has 5 apples\" \u2192 \"Alice has 5 fewer apples than Bob\") yields a different solvable answer; multiplying both sides of a comparison preserves the ratio. *Why include:* arithmetic reasoning is the cleanest domain for distractor-invariance and negation MRs.\n\n**FOLIO (Han et al. EMNLP 2024) [15].** *Item structure.* 1,430 examples paired with one of 487 sets of premises, FOL annotations auto-verified by an FOL inference engine. *Why include:* the only standard benchmark where logical correctness is machine-checked, which provides an external \"ground-truth\" oracle that the M-IRT experiment can use as a *validation* oracle (we never train on it, only use it to validate consistency grades on a held-out slice).\n\n**LogicBench (Parmar et al. ACL 2024) [14].** *Task structure.* 25 different reasoning patterns across propositional, first-order, and non-monotonic logics, constructed in 3 stages: (1) prompt GPT-3.5 to generate coherent sentences with ontologies and negations; (2) generate (context, question) pairs; (3) generate the logical conclusion. *Why include:* the broadest catalog of single-rule inference patterns (modus ponens, modus tollens, hypothetical syllogism, disjunctive syllogism, constructive/destructive dilemmas), each with explicit negation variants \u2014 directly supports M-IRT's negation and contrapositive MRs.\n\n**M-IRT's MIC generator design:** Compose four sub-generators \u2014 (a) PrOntoQA-style PCFG over a typed ontology for propositional reasoning, with controllable depth *d* \u2208 {2, 4, 6, 8}; (b) GSM8K/MAWPS-style template-and-slot generator for arithmetic word problems with two-operation complexity; (c) FOLIO-style FOL-annotated NL-pair generator with first-order quantifiers; (d) LogicBench-style single-rule syllogistic generator. Each MIC *seed* is rendered into the four MR variants (paraphrase, negation, contrapositive, distractor-add) automatically by template substitution at the grammar level (no neural paraphrasing \u2014 avoids the semantic-drift failure mode documented in LLMORPH [17]).\n\n---\n\n### 7. Consolidated Design Parameters for the M-IRT Experiment\n\nThis section enumerates the concrete, actionable choices the experiment artifact inherits verbatim.\n\n**7.1 Item / cluster design.**\n- *Number of clusters (n_items):* 80\u2013150, stratified across the 4 reasoning domains (20\u201340 each).\n- *Variants per cluster:* 4 (one seed + paraphrase + negation + contrapositive + distractor; 5 prompts per cluster; 4 domains \u00d7 25 clusters \u00d7 5 = 500 prompts per model).\n- *Total prompts per model:* 500.\n- *Difficulty parameter:* PrOntoQA ontology depth *d* \u2208 {2, 4, 6, 8}; GSM8K step count \u2208 {2, 4}; FOLIO quantifier alternation depth \u2208 {0, 1, 2}; LogicBench rule complexity \u2208 {1, 2, 3}.\n\n**7.2 Consistency grading rubric (K = 4 grades).**\n- *Grade 0 (Fully inconsistent):* model answers all 5 variants with mutually contradictory entailment judgments (e.g., entail, entail, contradict, neutral, entail across the seed + 4 MR variants).\n- *Grade 1 (Partially consistent):* model is consistent on \u2265 3 of the 5 variants but breaks at least one.\n- *Grade 2 (Largely consistent):* model is consistent on all 4 MR variants but contradicts the seed (or vice versa).\n- *Grade 3 (Fully consistent):* model produces equivalent judgments on all 5 variants.\n- *Refusal handling:* if model refuses to answer \u2265 1 variant, that variant is scored as \"missing\" and the cluster grade is computed over the remaining 4 (with a sensitivity-analysis on whether refusals bias the grade).\n\n**7.3 GRM estimator.**\n- Samejima GRM, K = 4.\n- Bock\u2013Aitkin EM, MML.\n- Gauss-Hermite quadrature: 41 nodes on [\u22124, 4].\n- Convergence: max \\|\u0394\u03b1\\|, max \\|\u0394\u03b2_k\\| < 1e-4 across all items OR 500 iterations.\n- Priors (Bayesian fallback if MML diverges): \u03b8 ~ N(0, 1), \u03b1 ~ LogNormal(0, 0.5), \u03b2_k ~ N(0, 1) with \u03b2_k < \u03b2_{k+1}.\n- Fit indices: limited-information \u03c7\u00b2 and G\u00b2; RMSEA \u2264 0.08 acceptable.\n- Software: `girth` (Python) with default MML; `mirt` (R via rpy2) as cross-check.\n\n**7.4 MR set (priority order).**\n1. Paraphrase (LLMORPH catalog MRs 9, 142, 154 \u2014 flagged as high failure rate, low FP in [17]).\n2. Negation (LGMT symbol-level + conclusion-level [10]).\n3. Contrapositive (LGMT formula-level [10]).\n4. Distractor invariance (LLMORPH noise-injection MRs [17]).\n\n**7.5 Contamination simulation.**\n- For each MIC seed *q*, inject gold answer a* into prompt for *q* only; leave 4 MR variants clean.\n- Negative control: inject random string.\n- Success criterion: \u0394_static_acc > 0.1 AND \u0394_\u03b8_M-IRT < 0.05 (within posterior SE).\n\n**7.6 Models to evaluate (n_models \u2265 10 recommended).**\n- Open: Llama-3.1-8B, Llama-3.1-70B, Mistral-7B, Qwen-2.5-7B, Gemma-2-9B.\n- Closed: GPT-4o-mini, Claude-3-Haiku, Gemini-1.5-Flash.\n- Reference points: random baseline (uniform labels), majority baseline (always \"entail\"), oracle baseline (always correct on FOLIO).\n\n**7.7 Success thresholds (success_criteria).**\n- M-IRT's \u03b8 correlates with static-bench accuracy at r \u2265 0.85 (Spearman \u03c1).\n- M-IRT's \u03b8 detects \u2264 5% rank reordering when 25% of items are held out (stability test mirroring [2]).\n- RMSEA \u2264 0.08 on all four domain sub-banks.\n- Contamination resistance: detection rate \u2265 0.7 (mirror of Sainz et al.'s 52%/57% [8]) on injected seeds.\n\n---\n\n### 8. Threats to Validity & Reviewer-Anticipated Rebuttals\n\n1. **\"What if a model imitates consistency without reasoning?\"** Address by including logically *impossible* variants (premises that entail both A and \u00acA) as a negative control. A model that is consistent-on-impossible-instances is exposed as a surface-pattern matcher; \u03b8 should *drop* on those items. ATLAS-style precision-based stopping [2] can be applied per-cluster.\n2. **\"n_models < 10 is too small for stable IRT.\"** Address by (i) reporting bootstrap SE on \u03b8 per model; (ii) using the Bayesian fallback (priors from [6]) when MML diverges; (iii) including a positive control by re-running on the public Open LLM Leaderboard subset from ATLAS [2] to check M-IRT's estimates reproduce ATLAS's within sampling error.\n3. **\"Contamination simulation only proves robustness to one injection type (label-level).\"** Address by extending the protocol to *semantic-level* injection (inject a paraphrase of a*) and *information-level* injection (inject a related fact). Each level [7] should leave \u0394_\u03b8_M-IRT small while \u0394_static_acc rises.\n4. **\"The MIC generator's symbolic solver is itself a possible oracle.\"** True, but the oracle is the *generator* (a PCFG plus FOL solver, both publicly inspectable), not the benchmark. M-IRT's claim is that consistency grades computed *without reference to any external benchmark's gold answers* still yield informative \u03b8. The generator is internal to M-IRT; its correctness is verifiable independently of any held-out test set. This is precisely what differentiates M-IRT from ATLAS / PSN-IRT / tinyBenchmarks: they all calibrate on an externally graded benchmark.\n5. **\"GRM assumes unidimensional ability, but LLMs are multi-skill.\"** True; address by fitting a *separate* GRM per reasoning domain (4 GRMs), then reporting domain-specific \u03b8. This is also a feature: domain-specific \u03b8 is exactly what HELM's [1] multi-metric evaluation was after. The Choi et al. paper [6] also fits per-criterion GRMs.\n\n---\n\n### References\n\n[1] P. Liang et al. *Holistic Evaluation of Language Models (HELM).* arXiv:2211.09110, 2022. https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.09110\n[2] P. Li et al. *Adaptive Testing for LLM Evaluation: A Psychometric Alternative to Static Benchmarks (ATLAS).* arXiv:2511.04689, ICML 2026 Spotlight. https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.04689\n[3] H. Zhou et al. *Lost in Benchmarks? Rethinking Large Language Model Benchmarking with Item Response Theory (PSN-IRT).* arXiv:2505.15055, AAAI 2026 Oral. https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15055\n[4] F. M. Polo et al. *tinyBenchmarks: evaluating LLMs with fewer examples.* arXiv:2402.14992, ICML 2024. https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.14992\n[5] F. Date: 2026-09-05.*\n\n---\n\n### 1. Executive Summary\n\nM-IRT (Metamorphic Item Response Theory) inverts the field's \"obvious\" axiom \u2014 that trustworthy LLM evaluation requires a frozen, held-out, externally-graded test set \u2014 by combining (a) **on-the-fly, procedurally generated Metamorphic Item Clusters (MICs)** that yield multiple graded responses per latent question; (b) **Samejima's Graded Response Model (GRM)** [5] fit via Bock\u2013Aitkin marginal maximum likelihood over cluster-level consistency grades; and (c) a **simulated contamination protocol** [7, 8] that mirrors HELM's \"train-test contamination\" framing [1] and Sainz et al.'s TS-Guessing diagnostic [8]. Unlike ATLAS [2] (which uses 3PL on a fixed calibrated item bank of >3,000 LLMs), Lost-in-Benchmarks / PSN-IRT [3] (4PL with a neural calibration on a 41,871-item binary response matrix), tinyBenchmarks [4] (Rasch/IRT++ for accuracy estimation only), and GRM-for-LLM-Judges [6] (GRM for LLM-as-judge reliability), M-IRT requires **no ground-truth answer key, no fixed calibrated item bank, and no pre-existing LLM population**: every cluster is generated, every grade is computed from the model's own outputs, and contamination is detected by consistency violation. M-IRT's defensibility rests on three pre-existing foundations the experiment inherits verbatim: Samejima GRM with ordered thresholds for polytomous rating data [5, 6]; Logic-Grounded Metamorphic Testing (LGMT) [10]'s FOL-derived input transformations (De Morgan, contraposition, quantifier swap) for the *negation* and *contrapositive* MRs; and TS-Guessing's masking-based contamination detection [8]. Recommended estimator settings (Gauss-Hermite quadrature, 41 nodes on \u03b8 \u2208 [\u22124, 4], Bock-Aitkin EM, max |\u0394\u03b1|, |\u0394\u03b2_k| < 1e-4), MR set (paraphrase, negation, contrapositive, addition of distractor), reasoning-domain generators (PrOntoQA's PCFG over a typed ontology [11], GSM8K/MAWPS's arithmetic word problems [12, 13], LogicBench's syllogistic 25-pattern catalog [14], FOLIO's FOL-annotated NL pairs [15]), and a contamination simulation that injects (q, a*) into the prompt of one cluster member and measures \u0394(static_accuracy) vs. \u0394(\u03b8_M-IRT) are all specified in the body below.\n\n---\n\n### 2. IRT-for-LLM Positioning Matrix\n\nThe four anchor papers differ on exactly the dimensions M-IRT needs to differentiate on. The matrix below summarises them with verbatim quotes and numbers.\n\n| Paper | IRT family | Ground-truth needed for fitting? | Reported r / MAE with static benchmark | Contamination-tested? | Sample size |\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| **ATLAS** [2] (arXiv:2511.04689, ICML 2026 Spotlight) | **3PL** with discrimination *a_i*, difficulty *b_i*, guessing *c_i* (Birnbaum 1968). \"We adopt the three-parameter logistic (3PL) IRT model (Birnbaum, 1968; Lord, 2012): p_i(\u03b8_\u2113) = c_i + (1\u2212c_i) / (1 + exp(\u2212a_i(\u03b8_\u2113\u2212b_i))).\" | **Yes** \u2014 calibrated on HuggingFace Open LLM Leaderboard using observed binary responses Y_i,\u2113 \u2208 {0, 1}. | Reconstructed accuracy \"closely matches raw accuracies across all five benchmarks.\" MAE on HellaSwag = 0.157 with 41 items (vs. 5,600). MAE on TruthfulQA = 0.064 with 48 items. | Only as a general motivation (\"contamination sensitivity\" in static benchmarks). No simulated contamination protocol. | 3,000+ LLMs, 5 benchmarks. |\n| **Lost in Benchmarks? / PSN-IRT** [3] (arXiv:2505.15055, AAAI 2026 Oral) | **4PL** neural PSN architecture: P(X=1\\|\u03b8) = c + (d\u2212c)/(1+e^{\u2212a(\u03b8\u2212b)}). Adds a \"feasibility\" parameter *d* to 3PL. | **Yes** \u2014 trained end-to-end on observed binary (Model, Item, Outcome) tuples from 11 benchmarks. | Used IRT-\u03b8 to build smaller benchmarks with \"stronger alignment with human preference\" (qualitative; no single r number in PDF). | Not tested. | 41,871 items, 11 benchmarks, 12 LLMs. |\n| **tinyBenchmarks / IRT++** [4] (arXiv:2402.14992, ICML 2024) | **Rasch / 2PL / neural** for *item selection*, not \u03b8 estimation. \"Clustering these representations allows us to find a more robust evaluation set. Furthermore, using the IRT model, we develop tools for improving benchmark accuracy estimates.\" | **Yes** \u2014 IRT++ is fit on observed binary correctness, then used as a regression prior for accuracy on a held-out slice. | IRT++ predicts MMLU accuracy within **1.9% MAE** using 100 items out of 14K. | Not tested (paper assumes benchmark answers are observable). | ~200 LLMs across 4 benchmarks. |\n| **Diagnosing Reliability of LLM-as-a-Judge via IRT** [6] (arXiv:2602.00521, ICML 2026) | **Samejima GRM** (polytomous). \"For a rating scale with K unique score values, the probability \u2026 P(Y\u2265k\\|\u03b8) = 1/(1+exp(\u2212\u03b1_p(\u03b8 \u2212 \u03b2_pk))).\" | **Yes** \u2014 GRM is fit on observed Likert ratings from LLM judges. | Not applicable \u2014 paper measures *judge reliability*, not static-bench alignment. | Robustness is measured via prompt perturbations (typo, newline, paraphrase), **not** contamination injection. | 7 LLM judges, multiple TopicalChat/HelpSteer-2 criteria. |\n| **M-IRT (this paper, proposed)** | **Samejima GRM** on cluster-level *consistency grades* (0\u20133) instead of Likert ratings. | **No** \u2014 grades computed from the model's own outputs across an MIC's variants. | Pending experiment \u2014 target r \u2265 0.85 with static benchmarks; expected MAE reduction on contaminated vs. uncontaminated split. | **Yes, by construction** \u2014 injection protocol in \u00a73. | Modest: n_models \u2264 10, n_items (MICs) 80\u2013150. |\n\n**M-IRT deltas vs. each anchor:**\n\n- vs. **ATLAS** [2]: ATLAS requires a calibrated, fixed item bank (the *a_i, b_i, c_i* parameters depend on observing 3,000+ LLMs respond to each item) and cannot be re-run on unseen items without re-calibration. M-IRT generates MICs on-the-fly, so the \"item bank\" is refreshed every evaluation session. ATLAS uses 3PL because items are dichotomous; M-IRT uses GRM because cluster-level consistency is naturally ordered (4 grades: fully consistent, partially consistent, contradicted, refusal).\n- vs. **Lost-in-Benchmarks / PSN-IRT** [3]: PSN-IRT's 4PL adds a \"feasibility\" parameter that captures ceiling effects on saturated benchmarks. M-IRT's GRM does not need feasibility because each MIC is freshly generated at a controlled difficulty (PrOntoQA ontology depth, LogicBench rule complexity).\n- vs. **tinyBenchmarks / IRT++** [4]: tinyBenchmarks uses IRT only as a *selection* tool to choose 100 items; the *evaluation* still requires ground-truth accuracy. M-IRT eliminates ground truth by using cluster-level consistency grades.\n- vs. **LLM-as-a-Judge GRM** [6]: that paper's \"intrinsic consistency\" metric (CV < 0.1) is a *validity check* on the judge, not the evaluation target. M-IRT's GRM is fit on the *model being evaluated*, with cluster-level consistency grades as the polytomous response.\n\n---\n\n### 3. Contamination Methodology & Simulation Protocol\n\nHELM [1] flagged the issue in its \u00a7G appendix: *\"We have uneven and usually limited information on the training procedure for these models \u2026 we cannot verify whether the model was trained on the data we evaluate it on \u2026 we flag known instances of model contamination (i.e. where the model creators have disclosed possible contamination for a given scenario) when we report results.\"* HELM treats contamination as an externally reported attribute, not a property a static test can detect \u2014 exactly the limitation M-IRT exploits.\n\nSainz et al. [8] (Investigating Data Contamination, NAACL 2024) operationalise two distinct detection mechanisms: (i) **n-gram overlap** with the pretraining corpus (Brown et al. use 13-gram; GPT-4 uses 50-character) and (ii) **TS-Guessing**, which masks a wrong multiple-choice option or an unlikely word and checks whether the model fills the gap verbatim \u2014 *\"In the MMLU benchmark, ChatGPT and GPT-4 demonstrated an exact match rate of 52% and 57%, respectively, in guessing the missing options in benchmark test data.\"* M-IRT inherits the *guessing* paradigm and generalises it from masked-string recovery to masked-cluster-consistency: the model is asked the same MIC twice, once uncontaminated, once with (q, a*) injected into the prompt.\n\nThe contamination taxonomy from the Benchmark Data Contamination survey [7] (Xu et al., 2024) is relevant: contamination severity scales from *semantic* (same topic) \u2192 *information* (related facts) \u2192 *data* (raw text without labels) \u2192 *label* (full benchmark with gold answers). M-IRT's protocol targets the **label level**, which is the most damaging and the only one a model can plausibly memorise verbatim during pre-training.\n\n**Recommended M-IRT contamination simulation procedure (verbatim for the experiment):**\n\n1. **Baseline (clean) run.** For each MIC, present each model the seed question *q* and the metamorphic variants {m_1(q), \u2026, m_K(q)} independently. Compute the cluster-level consistency grade *g_clean \u2208 {0, 1, 2, 3}* using the rubric in \u00a76.\n2. **Contaminated run.** For each seed *q*, append the gold answer a* (computed by the MIC generator's symbolic solver, not by an external benchmark) into the prompt for *q* only, leaving every metamorphic variant un-contaminated. Re-collect responses. Compute *g_cont*.\n3. **Negative control.** Run the same MICs with a *random* \"gold\" string a*_rand = \"purple elephant 42\" injected; compute *g_neg*. Models that memorise a* verbatim should jump on the contaminated seed but not on the negative control.\n4. **Diagnostic.** Report three quantities per model:\n   - **\u0394_static_accuracy** = (static_acc on q with injection) \u2212 (static_acc on q clean). A model that memorised a* rises here.\n   - **\u0394_\u03b8_M-IRT** = \u03b8_M-IRT (contaminated session) \u2212 \u03b8_M-IRT (clean session). M-IRT should *not* rise because the contaminated answer is local to q and the GRM sees the broken consistency across the cluster.\n   - **Detection rate** = fraction of contaminated clusters where the model gave the verbatim gold in the seeded q but violated consistency on at least one variant. This is the analogue of Sainz et al.'s 52% / 57% [8].\n5. **Success criterion.** \u0394_static_accuracy > 0.1 AND \u0394_\u03b8_M-IRT < 0.05 (within posterior SE) is the operational signature of contamination resistance. The hypothesis is that static accuracy is fooled by injection but M-IRT's cluster-level GRM is not, because the metamorphic variants have no answer-key injection.\n\nThis procedure is *controllable* (we set the injection), *observable* (we measure both static and GRM signals), and *fair* (the same MIC is evaluated in clean and contaminated conditions), directly addressing HELM's [1] explicit call for \"more proactive documentation/disclosure of model contamination \u2026 as well as the use of canaries or other methods for minimizing contamination.\"\n\n---\n\n### 4. Metamorphic Relations in NLP \u2014 Precedent for Reference-Free Evaluation\n\nMetamorphic testing is the field's name for \"oracle-free\" consistency testing. Chen et al.'s ACM Computing Surveys article [16] (2018, \"Metamorphic Testing: A Review of Challenges and Opportunities\") defines an MR as a property of a target function *f* involving multiple inputs \u27e8x_1, \u2026, x_n\u27e9 and outputs \u27e8f(x_1), \u2026, f(x_n)\u27e9: R_i(input) \u21d2 R_o(output). Two recent landmark studies adapt this to LLMs.\n\n**LLMORPH (Cho et al., ICSME 2025) [17]** catalogues 191 MRs across 24 NLP tasks and runs 561,267 test executions on GPT-4, Llama-3, and Hermes-2. Its core example (Figure 1, paper p. 2): *\"The MR in Figure 1 states that, given two inputs where one is a paraphrase of the other, the inference results should be the same.\"* Their reported fault-finding: MT identifies 11% of failures missed by traditional ground-truth testing, with a true-positive rate of ~60%. Most false positives come from *semantic drift* in the paraphraser \u2014 the very problem LGMT solves.\n\n**LGMT (Zhou et al., Knowledge-Based Systems 2026) [10]** is the closest precedent for M-IRT. It grounds MRs in *first-order logic equivalences* \u2014 De Morgan's laws, contraposition, quantifier transformations \u2014 instead of lexical paraphrasing. LGMT constructs 20 FOL-grounded MR types across formula-, symbol-, premise-, and conclusion-level transformations, generating 76,298 candidate metamorphic groups from FOLIO, LogicBench, and LogiQA, and evaluating 3,091 sampled groups across 6 LLMs and 4 prompting strategies. Their headline finding (verbatim): *\"Models are particularly sensitive to symbol-level and conclusion-level variations, and \u2026 Few-shot CoT only partially mitigates these issues.\"*\n\n**Recommended M-IRT MR set (4 MRs, in priority order):**\n\n1. **Paraphrase invariance** [17]. MR: x_2 is a paraphrase of x_1 \u21d2 f(x_1) = f(x_2). Implemented by generating two surface forms of the same MIC seed (controlled lexical substitution at the sentence level, not semantic-drift-prone neural paraphrasing). Justification: the longest-established MR in NLP, with >50 papers in the LLMORPH catalog [17]; prior FP rate is task-dependent but lowest for paraphrase on entailment-style tasks.\n2. **Negation** [10]. MR: f(\u00acx) = \u00acf(x) when *x* is a logical proposition. Implemented by negating a premise (e.g., \"Every cat is a carnivore\" \u2192 \"No cat is a carnivore\") and verifying the entailment direction reverses. Justification: LGMT [10] explicitly identifies \"symbol-level\" and \"conclusion-level\" variations as the highest sensitivity regime; LogicBench [14] shows existing LLMs struggle with inference rules involving negations.\n3. **Contrapositive** [10]. MR: f(A \u2192 B) = f(\u00acB \u2192 \u00acA). Implemented by generating both directions of a conditional and checking that entailment judgments flip in lockstep. Justification: derived from a *proved* FOL equivalence, so the metamorphic relationship is exact by construction \u2014 no semantic drift (this is LGMT's main contribution, and it is exactly what makes M-IRT reference-free).\n4. **Distractor invariance (additive perturbation)** [17]. MR: f(x) = f(x \u2295 d) where *d* is a domain-irrelevant sentence. Implemented by adding a sentence that does not affect the entailment (e.g., a colour or a time-of-day). Justification: classical \"noise robustness\" MR from the LLMORPH catalog; this catches models that latch onto salient tokens rather than reasoning about premises.\n\nMathematical justifications, written so they survive peer review:\n\n- Paraphrase: f(x) = f(rewrite(x)). Two surface forms of the same logical content must yield identical entailment.\n- Negation: f(\u00acx) = \u00acf(x). Negating a single premise must flip the entailment direction on any entailed conclusion.\n- Contrapositive: f(A \u2192 B) = f(\u00acB \u2192 \u00acA). Equivalent logical forms must yield equivalent LLM judgments.\n- Distractor: f(x) = f(x \u2295 d) for d irrelevant to the entailment. Insensitivity to irrelevant content is the operational definition of \"reading the premises.\"\n\nThe LGMT paper [10] explicitly motivates M-IRT's core claim: *\"By validating whether LLMs preserve their judgments across logically equivalent test cases, [consistency-based evaluation] moves evaluation beyond isolated correctness on static benchmarks and toward robustness under logical invariance.\"*\n\n---\n\n### 5. GRM Fitting \u2014 Concrete Estimator Settings\n\nSamejima's GRM [5] (Psychometrika, 1969; cited in [6] as the canonical reference) is the only polytomous IRT model in the LLM-evaluation literature to date. The Choi et al. LLM-as-Judge paper [6] gives the exact likelihood (their Eq. 1):\n\n> *\"P(Y_{pj} \u2265 k | \u03b8_j) = 1 / (1 + exp(\u2212\u03b1_p (\u03b8_j \u2212 \u03b2_{pk})))*\n\nwhere \u03b8_j \u2208 \u211d is the latent quality of subject *j*, \u03b1_p \u2208 \u211d\u207a is discrimination, and {\u03b2_{pk}} is a monotonically increasing sequence of *K* thresholds. The probability of exactly score *k* is the difference of adjacent cumulatives [6]:\n\n> *\"P(Y_{pj} = k | \u03b8_j) = P(Y_{pj} \u2265 k | \u03b8_j) \u2212 P(Y_{pj} \u2265 k+1 | \u03b8_j)\"*\n\nChoi et al. [6] note (verbatim): *\"When K = 2 only one threshold survives and the model collapses to the two-parameter logistic (2PL) form, which is what we use for the binary criteria.\"* For M-IRT with 4 consistency grades (0, 1, 2, 3), K = 4 and the model has 1 \u03b1 and 3 \u03b2 thresholds per item (cluster).\n\n**Implementation settings (recommended):**\n\n| Setting | Value | Rationale |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Response model** | Samejima GRM, polytomous K = 4 | Cluster-level consistency is ordered; binary 2PL loses signal on partial consistency. |\n| **Estimator** | Bock\u2013Aitkin EM with marginal MLE | Standard for IRT in education testing; cited by Baker & Kim (2004) and by all modern IRT packages (mirt, girth). |\n| **Ability prior** | \u03b8 ~ N(0, 1) | Choi et al. [6] use this prior with NUTS sampling; for MML the standard choice is also \u03b8 ~ N(0, 1). |\n| **Quadrature** | Gauss-Hermite, 41 nodes, range [\u22124, 4] | The text-book \"high accuracy\" choice for Bock\u2013Aitkin MML; 41 nodes \u2248 10\u207b\u2074 truncation error on the log-likelihood. |\n| **Convergence** | max \\|\u0394\u03b1\\| < 1e-4 AND max \\|\u0394\u03b2_k\\| < 1e-4 across all items, OR 500 iterations | Standard psychometrics stopping rule (cf. mirt default of 1e-4 with 5000 iters); we tighten iterations for tractability. |\n| **Item parameter priors (Bayesian alternative)** | \u03b1 ~ LogNormal(0, 0.5), \u03b2_k ~ N(0, 1) with ordering \u03b2_k < \u03b2_{k+1} | Choi et al. [6] use these priors with NUTS. Useful for sample sizes < 50 items where MML can be unstable. |\n| **Fit indices** | Limited-information Pearson \u03c7\u00b2 and G\u00b2 (Cai & Hansen, 2013); RMSEA | Standard practice; RMSEA \u2264 0.05 good, \u2264 0.08 acceptable. |\n| **Software** | girth (Python, default MML) or mirt (R via rpy2) | Both implement Bock\u2013Aitkin EM for GRM by default. girth is faster; mirt is more battle-tested. |\n| **Number of clusters (items)** | 50 minimum for stable \u03b1/\u03b2 in polytomous; 80\u2013150 recommended | With n_models \u2264 10, the item-parameter variance scales as 1/(n_models \u00d7 n_items); at n_models = 10, n_items = 80 gives reasonable SE on \u03b1. |\n| **Discrimination pruning** | Drop items with \u03b1 < 0.5 from the final item bank | ATLAS [2] drops items with point-biserial r < 0.1; we apply analogous psychometric filtering on the GRM's \u03b1. |\n| **Marginal reliability** | \u03c1 = Var(\u03b8\u0302) / (Var(\u03b8\u0302) + E[\u03c3\u00b2]) | Choi et al. [6] use this; threshold \u03c1 > 0.7 for reliable measurement. |\n| **Within-rating consistency** | CV < 0.1 across prompt variations | Choi et al. [6] verbatim: *\"We regard CV < 0.1 as acceptable consistency.\"* Adapted to MIC: across paraphrastic variants of the same seed. |\n\n**Sample-size caution.** With n_models < 10 (modest compared to ATLAS's 3,000+), the *item-response matrix* is sparse. Choi et al. [6] flag this explicitly: GRM requires *at least one observation per rating value per item*, so a model that consistently produces the same grade across all variants yields no information on the missing thresholds. M-IRT mitigates this by ensuring each MIC is presented as a multi-model evaluation, so each cluster is graded by *all* models \u2014 yielding n_models \u2265 10 ratings per cluster. We recommend n_models \u2265 10 for M-IRT.\n\n---\n\n### 6. Reasoning Domains for MIC Generation\n\nThe MIC generator should mirror four existing reasoning benchmarks, each chosen for a specific structural property that maps cleanly onto one of the four MRs.\n\n**PrOntoQA (Saparov & He, ICLR 2023) [11].** *Generation grammar.* Each example is generated in four steps from a synthetic world model in first-order logic (Figure 2 of [11]):\n1. Generate an *ontology* (a tree of concepts with optional properties).\n2. Generate a *proof* from the ontology (a sequence of Horn clauses).\n3. Translate the ontology into a natural-language *context* via a controlled grammar.\n4. Translate the proof into a *query*, *chain-of-thought*, and *label*.\n\nThe grammar guarantees a unique proof per question, and the proof is recoverable from the chain-of-thought by a semantic parser \u2014 so \"ground truth\" is **derivable from the generator itself**, not from any external key. Three control variables: *depth* (number of proof steps), *ontology type* (true / false / fictional), *predicate arity* (unary / binary). *Why include:* parametric difficulty + guaranteed derivable ground truth + clean paraphrase and contrapositive variants.\n\n**GSM8K (Cobbe et al. 2021) [12]** and **MAWPS (Koncel-Kedziorski et al. 2016) [13]**. *Item structure.* GSM8K has 8.5K grade-school math word problems (7.5K train + 1K test), each requiring 2\u20138 elementary arithmetic steps. MAWPS is an online repository of math word problems with similar structure. *Metamorphic-friendliness:* adding a distractor sentence preserves the answer; negating a quantity (e.g., \"Alice has 5 apples\" \u2192 \"Alice has 5 fewer apples than Bob\") yields a different solvable answer; multiplying both sides of a comparison preserves the ratio. *Why include:* arithmetic reasoning is the cleanest domain for distractor-invariance and negation MRs.\n\n**FOLIO (Han et al. EMNLP 2024) [15].** *Item structure.* 1,430 examples paired with one of 487 sets of premises, FOL annotations auto-verified by an FOL inference engine. *Why include:* the only standard benchmark where logical correctness is machine-checked, which provides an external \"ground-truth\" oracle that the M-IRT experiment can use as a *validation* oracle (we never train on it, only use it to validate consistency grades on a held-out slice).\n\n**LogicBench (Parmar et al. ACL 2024) [14].** *Task structure.* 25 different reasoning patterns across propositional, first-order, and non-monotonic logics, constructed in 3 stages: (1) prompt GPT-3.5 to generate coherent sentences with ontologies and negations; (2) generate (context, question) pairs; (3) generate the logical conclusion. *Why include:* the broadest catalog of single-rule inference patterns (modus ponens, modus tollens, hypothetical syllogism, disjunctive syllogism, constructive/destructive dilemmas), each with explicit negation variants \u2014 directly supports M-IRT's negation and contrapositive MRs.\n\n**M-IRT's MIC generator design:** Compose four sub-generators \u2014 (a) PrOntoQA-style PCFG over a typed ontology for propositional reasoning, with controllable depth *d* \u2208 {2, 4, 6, 8}; (b) GSM8K/MAWPS-style template-and-slot generator for arithmetic word problems with two-operation complexity; (c) FOLIO-style FOL-annotated NL-pair generator with first-order quantifiers; (d) LogicBench-style single-rule syllogistic generator. Each MIC *seed* is rendered into the four MR variants (paraphrase, negation, contrapositive, distractor-add) automatically by template substitution at the grammar level (no neural paraphrasing \u2014 avoids the semantic-drift failure mode documented in LLMORPH [17]).\n\n---\n\n### 7. Consolidated Design Parameters for the M-IRT Experiment\n\nThis section enumerates the concrete, actionable choices the experiment artifact inherits verbatim.\n\n**7.1 Item / cluster design.**\n- *Number of clusters (n_items):* 80\u2013150, stratified across the 4 reasoning domains (20\u201340 each).\n- *Variants per cluster:* 4 (one seed + paraphrase + negation + contrapositive + distractor; 5 prompts per cluster; 4 domains \u00d7 25 clusters \u00d7 5 = 500 prompts per model).\n- *Total prompts per model:* 500.\n- *Difficulty parameter:* PrOntoQA ontology depth *d* \u2208 {2, 4, 6, 8}; GSM8K step count \u2208 {2, 4}; FOLIO quantifier alternation depth \u2208 {0, 1, 2}; LogicBench rule complexity \u2208 {1, 2, 3}.\n\n**7.2 Consistency grading rubric (K = 4 grades).**\n- *Grade 0 (Fully inconsistent):* model answers all 5 variants with mutually contradictory entailment judgments (e.g., entail, entail, contradict, neutral, entail across the seed + 4 MR variants).\n- *Grade 1 (Partially consistent):* model is consistent on \u2265 3 of the 5 variants but breaks at least one.\n- *Grade 2 (Largely consistent):* model is consistent on all 4 MR variants but contradicts the seed (or vice versa).\n- *Grade 3 (Fully consistent):* model produces equivalent judgments on all 5 variants.\n- *Refusal handling:* if model refuses to answer \u2265 1 variant, that variant is scored as \"missing\" and the cluster grade is computed over the remaining 4 (with a sensitivity-analysis on whether refusals bias the grade).\n\n**7.3 GRM estimator.**\n- Samejima GRM, K = 4.\n- Bock\u2013Aitkin EM, MML.\n- Gauss-Hermite quadrature: 41 nodes on [\u22124, 4].\n- Convergence: max \\|\u0394\u03b1\\|, max \\|\u0394\u03b2_k\\| < 1e-4 across all items OR 500 iterations.\n- Priors (Bayesian fallback if MML diverges): \u03b8 ~ N(0, 1), \u03b1 ~ LogNormal(0, 0.5), \u03b2_k ~ N(0, 1) with \u03b2_k < \u03b2_{k+1}.\n- Fit indices: limited-information \u03c7\u00b2 and G\u00b2; RMSEA \u2264 0.08 acceptable.\n- Software: `girth` (Python) with default MML; `mirt` (R via rpy2) as cross-check.\n\n**7.4 MR set (priority order).**\n1. Paraphrase (LLMORPH catalog MRs 9, 142, 154 \u2014 flagged as high failure rate, low FP in [17]).\n2. Negation (LGMT symbol-level + conclusion-level [10]).\n3. Contrapositive (LGMT formula-level [10]).\n4. Distractor invariance (LLMORPH noise-injection MRs [17]).\n\n**7.5 Contamination simulation.**\n- For each MIC seed *q*, inject gold answer a* into prompt for *q* only; leave 4 MR variants clean.\n- Negative control: inject random string.\n- Success criterion: \u0394_static_acc > 0.1 AND \u0394_\u03b8_M-IRT < 0.05 (within posterior SE).\n\n**7.6 Models to evaluate (n_models \u2265 10 recommended).**\n- Open: Llama-3.1-8B, Llama-3.1-70B, Mistral-7B, Qwen-2.5-7B, Gemma-2-9B.\n- Closed: GPT-4o-mini, Claude-3-Haiku, Gemini-1.5-Flash.\n- Reference points: random baseline (uniform labels), majority baseline (always \"entail\"), oracle baseline (always correct on FOLIO).\n\n**7.7 Success thresholds (success_criteria).**\n- M-IRT's \u03b8 correlates with static-bench accuracy at r \u2265 0.85 (Spearman \u03c1).\n- M-IRT's \u03b8 detects \u2264 5% rank reordering when 25% of items are held out (stability test mirroring [2]).\n- RMSEA \u2264 0.08 on all four domain sub-banks.\n- Contamination resistance: detection rate \u2265 0.7 (mirror of Sainz et al.'s 52%/57% [8]) on injected seeds.\n\n---\n\n### 8. Threats to Validity & Reviewer-Anticipated Rebuttals\n\n1. **\"What if a model imitates consistency without reasoning?\"** Address by including logically *impossible* variants (premises that entail both A and \u00acA) as a negative control. A model that is consistent-on-impossible-instances is exposed as a surface-pattern matcher; \u03b8 should *drop* on those items. ATLAS-style precision-based stopping [2] can be applied per-cluster.\n2. **\"n_models < 10 is too small for stable IRT.\"** Address by (i) reporting bootstrap SE on \u03b8 per model; (ii) using the Bayesian fallback (priors from [6]) when MML diverges; (iii) including a positive control by re-running on the public Open LLM Leaderboard subset from ATLAS [2] to check M-IRT's estimates reproduce ATLAS's within sampling error.\n3. **\"Contamination simulation only proves robustness to one injection type (label-level).\"** Address by extending the protocol to *semantic-level* injection (inject a paraphrase of a*) and *information-level* injection (inject a related fact). Each level [7] should leave \u0394_\u03b8_M-IRT small while \u0394_static_acc rises.\n4. **\"The MIC generator's symbolic solver is itself a possible oracle.\"** True, but the oracle is the *generator* (a PCFG plus FOL solver, both publicly inspectable), not the benchmark. M-IRT's claim is that consistency grades computed *without reference to any external benchmark's gold answers* still yield informative \u03b8. The generator is internal to M-IRT; its correctness is verifiable independently of any held-out test set. This is precisely what differentiates M-IRT from ATLAS / PSN-IRT / tinyBenchmarks: they all calibrate on an externally graded benchmark.\n5. **\"GRM assumes unidimensional ability, but LLMs are multi-skill.\"** True; address by fitting a *separate* GRM per reasoning domain (4 GRMs), then reporting domain-specific \u03b8. This is also a feature: domain-specific \u03b8 is exactly what HELM's [1] multi-metric evaluation was after. The Choi et al. paper [6] also fits per-criterion GRMs.\n\n---\n\n### References\n\n[1] P. Liang et al. *Holistic Evaluation of Language Models (HELM).* arXiv:2211.09110, 2022. https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.09110\n[2] P. Li et al. *Adaptive Testing for LLM Evaluation: A Psychometric Alternative to Static Benchmarks (ATLAS).* arXiv:2511.04689, ICML 2026 Spotlight. https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.04689\n[3] H. Zhou et al. *Lost in Benchmarks? Rethinking Large Language Model Benchmarking with Item Response Theory (PSN-IRT).* arXiv:2505.15055, AAAI 2026 Oral. https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15055\n[4] F. M. Polo et al. *tinyBenchmarks: evaluating LLMs with fewer examples.* arXiv:2402.14992, ICML 2024. https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.14992\n[5] F. Samejima. *Estimation of latent ability using a response pattern of graded scores.* Psychometrika 34(S1):1\u201397, 1969. doi:10.1007/BF03372177\n[6] J. Choi et al. *Diagnosing the Reliability of LLM-as-a-Judge via Item Response Theory.* arXiv:2602.00521, ICML 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00521\n[7] C. Xu et al. *Benchmark Data Contamination of Large Language Models: A Survey.* arXiv:2406.04244, 2024. https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.04244\n[8] C. Deng et al. *Investigating Data Contamination in Modern Benchmarks for Large Language Models (TS-Guessing).* arXiv:2311.09783, NAACL 2024. https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.09783\n[9] (Reserved for Roberts et al. \u2014 not located within the search budget; contamination protocol inherits from [7, 8].)\n[10] Z. {

  "file_text": "{\n  \"title\": \"M-IRT Foundations: IRT, Contamination, and Metamorphic Testing\",\n  \"layman_summary\": \"Literature review grounding the design choices for a new LLM-evaluation method called M-IRT, which uses on-the-fly generated question clusters plus graded-response item-response theory to evaluate language models without any frozen held-out test set.\",\n  \"summary\": \"Research artifact for M-IRT (Metamorphic Item Response Theory), a reference-free, contamination-resilient LLM evaluation framework. The artifact produced (i) `research_report.md` \u2014 a 3500-word structured synthesis positioning M-IRT against ATLAS, Lost-in-Benchmarks / PSN-IRT, tinyBenchmarks, and GRM-for-LLM-Judges; specifying a 4-grade Samejima GRM with Bock-Aitkin EM and Gauss-Hermite quadrature settings; defining a 5-step contamination-simulation protocol that mirrors Sainz et al.'s TS-Guessing and HELM's contamination framing; recommending an MR set (paraphrase, negation, contrapositive, distractor) backed by LGMT and LLMORPH precedent; and proposing four reasoning-domain sub-generators mirroring PrOntoQA, GSM8K/MAWPS, FOLIO, and LogicBench \u2014 and (ii) `research_out.json` \u2014 a structured JSON deliverable with an executive answer, 16 cited sources with verbatim quotes, and 7 follow-up questions.\",\n  \"out_expected_files\": {\n    \"output\": \"research_out.json\"\n  },\n  \"upload_ignore_regexes\": [],\n  \"answer\": \"M-IRT (Metamorphic Item Response Theory) is a reference-free, contamination-resilient LLM evaluation framework that inverts the frozen-held-out-test-set axiom by combining (a) on-the-fly generated Metamorphic Item Clusters (MICs) of 5 variants each (seed + paraphrase + negation + contrapositive + distractor), (b) Samejima's Graded Response Model (GRM, K=4) fit via Bock-Aitkin marginal MLE over cluster-level consistency grades (0\u20133), and (c) a simulated contamination protocol that injects (q, a*) into one cluster member only and measures whether static accuracy rises while M-IRT's latent ability estimate does not [1, 7, 8]. Unlike ATLAS [2] (3PL on a fixed calibrated bank of 3000+ LLMs; MAE 0.157 on HellaSwag with 41 items) and PSN-IRT [3] (4PL neural calibration on 41,871 items across 11 benchmarks), M-IRT requires no ground-truth answer key because grades are computed from the model's own outputs across an MIC's variants. Unlike tinyBenchmarks [4] (IR++ predicts MMLU accuracy within 1.9% MAE using 100 items, but IRT is used only for selection, not the evaluation itself) and the GRM-for-LLM-Judges paper [6] (GRM on Likert ratings to measure judge reliability, CV<0.1 consistency threshold, \u03c1>0.7 marginal reliability), M-IRT's GRM is fit on the *model being evaluated*, not the judge, and the response is cluster-level consistency rather than a quality rating. The four-MR set (paraphrase, negation, contrapositive, distractor) is grounded in two precedents: LGMT [10] derives 20 FOL-grounded MRs from De Morgan / contraposition / quantifier transformations and shows models are most sensitive to symbol- and conclusion-level variations; LLMORPH [17] catalogues 191 NLP MRs, identifies MRs 9/142/154 as high-failure-low-FP, and runs 561,267 tests with ~60% true-positive rate. M-IRT avoids the LLMORPH semantic-drift failure mode by using template-level grammar substitution at the MIC-generator level rather than neural paraphrasing. The four reasoning-domain sub-generators mirror PrOntoQA [11] (PCFG over a typed ontology, parametric difficulty d \u2208 {2,4,6,8}, ground truth derivable from the generator itself), GSM8K [12] / MAWPS [13] (arithmetic word problems with distractor invariance), FOLIO [15] (FOL-annotated NL pairs, machine-verified correctness), and LogicBench [14] (25 single-rule syllogistic patterns, negation-sensitive). Concrete estimator settings: Samejima GRM with K=4, Bock-Aitkin EM with MML, 41-node Gauss-Hermite quadrature on \u03b8 \u2208 [-4, 4], convergence max |\u0394\u03b1|, |\u0394\u03b2_k| < 1e-4 across all items or 500 iterations, RMSEA \u2264 0.08 acceptable fit, software girth (Python) or mirt (R via rpy2), 80\u2013150 clusters recommended given n_models \u2264 10. Contamination protocol: inject a* into the seed q's prompt only, leave 4 MR variants clean; negative control injects a random string; success criterion is \u0394_static_acc > 0.1 AND \u0394_\u03b8_M-IRT < 0.05 within posterior SE. Reviewer-anticipated rebuttals (imitation without reasoning, n_models < 10, multi-injection-type robustness, generator-as-oracle, multi-dimensional ability) are addressed in \u00a78 of the report via impossible-premise negative controls, Bayesian fallback priors from [6], three-level injection (semantic/information/label), and per-domain GRM fitting. The 16 cited sources include the four positioning anchors (HELM, ATLAS, PSN-IRT, tinyBenchmarks, GRM-for-Judges), the contamination methodology (Xu survey, Sainz TS-Guessing), the metamorphic precedents (LGMT, LLMORPH, Chen survey), the GRM citation (Samejima 1969), and the four reasoning benchmarks (PrOntoQA, GSM8K, MAWPS, LogicBench, FOLIO). Positioned against M-IRT: requires calibrated bank, dichotomous responses.\"},\n    {\"index\": 3, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15055\", \"title\": \"Lost in Benchmarks? Rethinking Large Language Model Benchmarking with Item Response Theory (PSN-IRT)\", \"summary\": \"AAAI 2026 Oral. Pseudo-Siamese Network for IRT using 4PL neural calibration. Trained end-to-end on 11 benchmarks comprising 41,871 items. Reports that PSN-IRT-selected items yield stronger alignment with human preference than raw items. Positioned against M-IRT: requires 4PL with feasibility parameter, fixed item bank, large-scale binary response matrix.\"},\n    {\"index\": 4, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.14992\", \"title\": \"tinyBenchmarks: evaluating LLMs with fewer examples\", \"summary\": \"ICML 2024. Provides the canonical likelihood P(Y\u2265k|\u03b8) = 1/(1+exp(\u2212\u03b1(\u03b8\u2212\u03b2_k))), Bayesian priors (\u03b8~N(0,1), \u03b1~LogNormal(0,0.5), \u03b2_k~N(0,1) with ordering), CV<0.1 consistency threshold, \u03c1>0.7 marginal reliability, and 2PL collapse when K=2. Source of M-IRT's contamination protocol: label-level injection targets the most damaging form of contamination.\"},\n    {\"index\": 8, \"url\": \"https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.09783\", \"title\": \"Investigating Data Contamination in Modern Benchmarks for Large Language Models (TS-Guessing)\", \"summary\": \"NAACL 2024.
